Filter

Similarly, given a “predicate” function
f
from
s
to
t+\varepsilon
(the streaming version of
t\;\texttt{option}
),
we can transform an incoming stream of
s^{\star}
to include just the transformed elements which pass the filter.

We can then recover a traditional predicate-based filter by lifting a predicate
f
that takes an in-memory
s
to
Bool

to a streaming function
s -> s + Eps
with
liftP
. This program simply waits for its argument to arrive, then
applies the predicate to the in-memory
s
.

⬇

fun

fold

[
s
,
t
]

<
f

:

{
t
}(
s
)

->

t
>{
acc

:

t
}(
xs

:

s
*)

:

t

=

case

xs

of

nil

=>

{
acc
}

|

y

::

ys

=>

wait

f
{
acc
}(
y
)

as

acc
’

do

fold
{
acc
’}(
ys
)

end

Fold

\lambda^{\text{ST}}
can express both
running folds
, which output a
stream of all their intermediate
states, and
functional folds
, which output only the final state.
Since functional folds that return only the final state
cannot be given this rich type in traditional stream processing languages (for the same reason as the
head
and
tail
functions),
we present one here. See Appendix
B.1
for
discussion of a running fold; the code is similar except that it
outputs
y
at every step.

The
fold
transducer maintains an in-memory accumulator of type
t
; this gets
updated by a streaming
step function
f : {t}(s) -> t
that takes the state
t
and
the new element
s
and produces a
t
. The whole fold takes a stream

xs
of type
s^{\star}
and an initial accumulator value
y:t
, and it eventually
produces the final state
t
. As for
map
, the code for
fold
is
very similar to the traditional functional program: the only distinction is
the inclusion of
wait
s to marshal data into memory.

⬇

fun

head

[
s
]

(
xs

:

s
*)

:

Eps

+

s

=

case

xs

of

nil

=>

inl
(
sink
)

|

y

::

_

=>

inr
(
y
)

Singletons, Head, Tail

In the homogeneous model, stream types are always conceptually unbounded.
But in many practical situations, a stream will only be expected to contain
a single element—a constraint that cannot be expressed with homogeneous
streams. Using stream types, we can write stream transformers that
are statically known to only produce a single output. For example, the
“head” function
is trivially expressible in the same manner as head on lists, as shown
on the right.

(Exercise:
try writing the term for
tail
on star streams. This
requires a use of
wait
and an accumulator argument like in

fold
. )

⬇

fun

averageSingle

(
run

:

Int

.

Int
*)

:

Int

=

let

(
x
;
xs
)

=

run

in

let

(
sm
,
len
)

=

(
sum
(
xs
),

length
(
xs
))

in

wait

x
,
sm
,
len

do

{(
x

+

sm
)

/

(1

+

len
)}

end

fun

averageAbove
{
t

:

Int
}(
xs

:

Int
*)

:

Int
*

=

map
<
averageSingle
>(
thresh
{
t
}(
xs
))

Brightness Levels

The structured communication protocol from the brightness-levels example in Section
2

can be encoded as the type
\left(\texttt{Int}\cdot\texttt{Int}^{\star}\right)^{\star}
:
a stream of nonempty sequences of
Int
s, representing “runs” of
light levels greater than some threshold.
Given such a stream, writing a program to compute the averages is easy:
we just
map
an average operation—taking
\texttt{Int}\cdot\texttt{Int}^{\star}
to
Int
—over the incoming stream to produce a
stream
\texttt{Int}^{\star}
of averages. The per-run average
operation,
averageSingle
, is defined by computing its sum and length
in parallel, waiting for the results, then dividing the sum (plus the
first element) by the length (plus one).

Notice that, since each run is statically known to have at
least one element,
averageSingle
can omit the error handling that, with a
homogeneous stream type, would be needed to avoid a potential divide by zero.
By contrast, with a homogeneous stream type like
\left(\texttt{Start}+\texttt{Int}+\texttt{End}\right)^{\star}
, this operation would need to be written in a
low-level, more stateful manner, remembering the current run of

Int
s
until an
End
event arrives, averaging, and handling the divide-by-zero
error which could in principle (although not in practice) occur if no
Int
s
arrived between a
Start
and an
End
.

⬇

fun

thresh
{
t

:

Int
}(
xs

:

Int
*)

:

(
Int

.

Int
*)*

=

case

xs

of

nil

=>

nil

|

y

::

ys

=>

wait

y

do

if

{
y

>

t
}

then

let

(
run
;
rest
)

=

spanGt
{
t
}(
ys
)

in

({
y
};
run
)

::

thresh
{
t
}(
rest
)

else

thresh
{
t
}(
ys
)

end

The thresholding operation
thresh
, which
takes
\texttt{Int}^{\star}
and
produces the runs of elements above the threshold
\left(\texttt{Int}\cdot\texttt{Int}^{\star}\right)^{\star}
, is straightforward.
Whenever the incoming stream goes above the threshold
t
, we collect all
of the subsequent elements into a run, emit it, and recurse down the rest of the stream.
This uses an operation
spanGt : {Int} (Int*) -> Int . Int*
that returns the initial “span” of
elements above
t
, followed by the rest of the stream.
It’s important to note that this program is completely
non-blocking: as soon as the first element above
t
arrives, it is
forwarded along, as are all subsequent elements until the stream drops below
t
.
By contrast with homogeneously typed streaming languages, delta’s
type safety guarantees
that
thresh

does in fact
output a stream that adheres to the protocol, and (2)
any downstream transformer does not have to replicate this parsing logic.

The complete program, first calling
thresh
, and then mapping
averageSingle
over the stream of runs,
is
averageAbove
.

Side Outputs & Error Handling

A common streaming idiom is the use of “side outputs” for reporting errors.
In languages that support this idiom, operations include extra output
streams where error messages are sent as they
arise at runtime. These side outputs are always a second-class
mechanism: the error streams cannot be transformed or used in a manner other
than dumping them to a log somewhere.
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
provides a first-class account of
side outputs, encoding them as a parallel output type. A function
s\to t

that may produce errors of type
e
can have type
s\to t\|e^{\star}
.
Alternately, errors can be handled inline in the traditional functional
way, using a sum type
s+e
.

Partitioning and Merging

Partitioning is a crucial streaming idiom where a single stream of
data is split into two or more parallel streams to be
routed to different downstream processing nodes, thus
exposing parallelism and increasing potential throughput.
Appendix
B.2
shows how two different partitioning
strategies can be implemented in
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
.
First is a
round-robin partitioner
,
which fairly partitions an incoming stream of type

s^{\star}
into a parallel pair of streams
s^{\star}\|s^{\star}

by sending the first element to the left branch, the second to the
right, and so on.

Second is a
decision-based partitioner
, which routes a stream of type
s^{\star}

one direction or another into an output stream of type
t^{\star}\|r^{\star}
based on the result
of a function from
s
to
t+r
.

Windowing and Punctuation

Windowing
is another core concept in stream processing systems, where
aggregation operations like
moving averages or sums are defined over “windows”—groupings of consecutive
events, gathered together into a set. In
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
, these transformers are just

map
s over a stream whose elements are windows. Given a per-window
aggregation transformer
f
from an individual window

s^{\star}
to a result type

t
, plus a “windowing strategy”
win
which takes a stream
r^{\star}

and turns it into a stream of windows
s^{\star\star}
, we can write
the windowed operation as
xs : r
⋆
|-
map<f>(win(xs)) : t
⋆
. Appendix
B.3
defines both
sliding and tumbling size-based window operators,
as well as punctuation-based windowing, where windows are delimited by
punctuation marks inserted into the stream.

6.
Related Work

Streams as a programming abstraction have their sources in early work
in the programming languages
(
Kahn 1974
;
Burge 1975
;
Stephens 1997
;
Thies et al. 2002
)

and database
(
Abadi et al. 2003
;
Abadi et al. 2005
;
Chandrasekaran et al. 2003
;
Madden et al. 2002
;
Arasu et al. 2004
;
Arasu et al. 2003
;
Arasu et al. 2006
)

communities.
Though streams have mostly been viewed as homogeneous
sequences, more interesting treatments have also been proposed.
For example, streams in the database literature are sometimes viewed as time-varying relations,
while the PL community has produced formalisms like process calculi and functional reactive programming.
To our knowledge, ours is first type system for
streams capturing both (1) heterogeneous patterns of
events over time and (2) combinations of parallel and sequential
data.

Sequential, homogeneous streams and dataflow programs
.
Traditionally, streams have been viewed in the PL community as coinductive sequences
(
Burge 1975
)
: a stream of
A
has a single (co)constructor,
\texttt{cocons}:\texttt{Stream}A\to(A\times\texttt{Stream }A)
and acts as a lazily evaluated infinite list.
In particular, this is the setting of traditional
dataflow programming

(
Stephens 1997
)
.
One major challenge in reasoning about dataflow over sequential
streams is the nondeterminism arising from operators
whose output may depend on the order in
which events arrive on multiple input streams.
Kahn’s seminal “process networks”
(
Kahn 1974
)

(including their restriction to synchronous networks
(
Lee and Messerschmitt 1987
;
Thies et al. 2002
;
Benveniste et al. 2003
)
)
avoid this problem by allowing only
blocking reads
of
messages on FIFO queues.
In contrast, the semantics of
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
leverages its type structure to
guarantee deterministic parallel processing
without
blocking in many cases.
For example, in the context of a
T-Let
rule,
if the type system can detect statically that a transformer is using
two parallel streams safely, it can read from them
simultaneously.

Partitioned streams
.
Building on streams as homogeneous sequences,
modern stream processing systems such as
Flink
(
Foundation 2019a
;
Carbone et al. 2015
)
,
Spark Streaming
(
Foundation 2019d
;
Zaharia et al. 2013
)
,
Samza
(
Foundation 2019c
;
Noghabi et al. 2017
)
,
Arc
(
Kroll et al. 2019
)
,
and Storm
(
Foundation 2019e
)

support
dynamic partitioning
: a stream type can define one stream
with many parallel substreams,
where the number of substreams and assignment of data to substreams is determined at runtime.
The type
Stream t
in these systems
is implicitly a parallel composition of homogeneous streams:
\texttt{t}^{\star}\|\cdots\|\texttt{t}^{\star}
.
Unlike in
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
, these parallel substreams cannot have more general types.

Some which papers which attempt to build very general compile targets for stream
processing support parallelism in only restricted ways. For example,
Brooklet
(
Soulé et al. 2010
)
and the DON Calculus
(
Dexter et al. 2022
)

support data parallelism only as an optimization pass in limited cases.
This is because stream partitioning does not in general
preserve the semantics of the source program and can introduce
undesirable nondeterminism
(
Mamouras et al. 2019
;
Schneider et al. 2013
;
Hirzel et al. 2014
)
.
While
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
does not support dynamic partitioning, we hope to address it in future work; see Section
7
.

Streams as time-varying relations
.
In the database literature,
streams are often viewed as relations (sets of tuples) that vary over time.
Stream management systems in the early 2000s pioneered this paradigm,
including
Aurora
(
Abadi et al. 2003
)
and
Borealis
(
Abadi et al. 2005
)
,
TelegraphCQ
(
Chandrasekaran et al. 2003
)
and
CACQ
(
Madden et al. 2002
)
,
and STREAM
(
Arasu et al. 2004
)
.
A time-varying relation can be viewed as either a function
from timestamps to finite relations
or an infinite set of timestamped values;
this correspondence was elegantly exploited by early streaming query languages such as CQL
(
Arasu et al. 2003
;
Arasu et al. 2006
)

and remains popular today
(
Jain et al. 2008
;
Begoli et al. 2019
)
.
Time-varying relations can be expressed in
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
using
Kleene star and concatenation:
a relation of tuples of type
T
timestamped by
Time
can be expressed as

\left(\texttt{T}^{\star}\cdot\texttt{{Time}}\right)^{\star}
.
We can also express the common pattern where parallel streams
are synchronized by a single timestamp (again, modulo dynamic
partitioning) with types like

\left(\left(\texttt{T}^{\star}\|\texttt{T}^{\star}\right)\cdot\texttt{{Time}}\right)^{\star}
.
Each
Time
event is a punctuation mark containing the
timestamp of the prior set of
tuples
(
Tucker et al. 2003b
;
Johnson et al. 2005
)
.
Traditional systems include separate APIs for
operations that
modify punctuation (e.g., a
delay
function that increments timestamps);
whereas in our system they are ordinary stream operators and
punctuation markers are ordinary events.

Streams as pomsets
.
A sweet spot between the homogeneous sequential and relational
viewpoints is found in prior work treating streams as

pomsets
(partially ordered multisets)
(
Alur et al. 2021
;
Mamouras et al. 2019
;
Kallas et al. 2020
;
Kallas et al. 2022
;
Kappé et al. 2019
)
,
inspired by work in concurrency
theory
(
Mazurkiewicz 1986
;
Diekert and Rozenberg 1995
)
.
In a pomset,
data items may be completely ordered (a sequence), completely unordered (a bag), or somewhere in between.
Some recent works have proposed pomset-based types for streams
(
Mamouras et al. 2019
;
Alur et al. 2021
)
, but their types do not support concatenation
and do not come with type
systems
—programs must be shown to be
well typed semantically, rather than via syntactic typing
rules.

Functional reactive programming (FRP)

(
Elliott and Hudak 1997
)

treats programs as incremental, reactive state machines
written using functional combinators.
The fundamental abstraction is a “signal”:
a time-varying value
Sig(A) = Time -> A
.
Work on type systems for FRP has used modal and substructural types

(
Bahr et al. 2019
;
Bahr et al. 2021
;
Cave et al. 2014
;
Krishnaswami 2013
)

to guarantee properties like causality, productivity,
and space leak freedom. While our type system is not

designed
to address these issues, it does incidentally have bearing on
them. For one, our incremental semantics
demonstrates that
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
’s type system enforces causality: since outputs that have been incrementally emitted cannot be retracted or changed,
the type system must ensure that past outputs cannot depend on future inputs. Similarly,
potential space leaks can be detected
statically by checking that only bounded-sized types are buffered using

wait
or the buffering built into the left rules for sums and star.
Our current calculus does not guarantee productivity (new inputs must
eventually produce new outputs),
but in Section
7

we discuss how to remedy this by imposing guardedness conditions on
recursive calls.

Jeffrey 2012
permits the type of a signal to vary over time, using dependent types
inspired by Linear Temporal Logic

(
Pnueli 1977
)
.
This system includes an
until
type that behaves like our
concatenation type: a signal of type
A\,U\,B
is a signal of type
A
,
followed by a signal of type
B
.
However, unlike parallel streams in our setting, time updates in
steps, discretely; i.e., parallel signals all present new values together, at the same time.
Concurrently with our work,
Bahr and Møgelberg 2023
proposes a modal type system to weaken the synchronicity assumption;
however, it still treats signals as homogeneous: the type of data cannot change over time.
Lastly,
Paykin et al. 2016
develop a modal type system which expresses low-level event handlers.
These are also purely synchronous, and the programs are written as event handlers
as opposed to high-level “batch” processors.

Stream Runtime Verification (SRV)
aims, broadly, to monitor streams
at runtime and provide boolean or numerical “triggers” that fire when they
satisfy some specification. Many RV projects like LOLA

(
D’Angelo et al. 2005
)
, HLola
(
Ceresa et al. 2020
)
, RTLola
(
Faymonville et al. 2019
)
, Striver
(
Gorostiaga and Sánchez 2021
)
,
HStriver
(
Gorostiaga and Sánchez 2021
)
also provide high-level, declarative specification languages
for writing such monitors. Because these languages often use regular expressions or LTL
as a formalism, they often bear a resemblance to our stream types.
Despite this similarity, our goals and methods are quite different. Unlike
the dynamically-checked specifications of SRV, the types in delta are static guarantees:
a stream program of type
s
necessarily produces a stream of type
s
.

Streaming with Laziness
.
It is folklore in the Haskell community that a “sufficiently lazy” list program
can be run as a streaming program using a clever trick with lazy IO

(
Kiselyov 2012
)

(
Trilla 2024
)
. This
“sufficient laziness” condition is syntactically brittle, and requires an expert Haskell
programmer to carefully ensure that all functions involved are lazy in the just the
right way. Indeed, many Haskell programmers instead reach for combinator libraries like Pipes
(
Gonzalez 2022
)

FoldL
(
Gonzalez 2024
)
, Conduit
(
Snoyman 2023
)
, Streamly
(
Technologies 2023
)
, and others to ensure their programs have a streaming semantics.
In delta, the type system takes care of this for you: all well-typed programs can be given a streaming
semantics. Moreover, the
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
semantics gives a direct account of how pure
functions execute incrementally as state machines, as opposed to
the way that Haskell’s non-strict semantics incidentally yields streaming behavior when combined with Lazy IO.

Session types and process calculi
.
Another large body of work with similar vision is
session types for process calculi
(
Honda et al. 2008
)
, where types describe complex
sequential protocols between communicating processes as they
evolve through time. A main difference from our work is that
the session type of a process describes the
protocol
for its
communications with other processes—i.e., the sequence of sends and
receives on different channels—while the stream type of a
\lambda^{\text{ST}}

program describes only the data that it communicates.
Indeed, a stream transformer might display many
patterns of communication with downstream transformers:
it can run in “batch mode”—sending exactly one output after accepting
all available input—or in a sequence smaller steps, sending along
partial outputs as it receives partial inputs.
Also, a single channel in a process calculus
cannot carry parallel substreams: all events in a channel are ordered relative to each other.
Recently,
Frumin et al. 2022
proposed a session-types
interpretation of BI
that uses the bunched structure very
differently from
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
.
In particular, processes of type
A*B
and
A\wedge B
both behave
semantically like a process of type
A
in parallel with a process of
type
B
, while, in

\lambda^{\text{ST}}
,
s\cdot t
and
s\|t
describe very different streams.

Concurrent Kleene Algebras and regular expression types
.
Stream types are partly inspired by Concurrent Kleene Algebras
(CKAs)
(
Hoare et al. 2009
)
and related syntaxes for pomset
languages
(
Kappé et al. 2019
)
, but we are apparently the first to use these formalisms as
types
in a programming
language rather than as a tool for reasoning about concurrency.
In particular, traditional applications of Kleene algebra such as NetKAT
(
Anderson et al. 2014
)
and Concurrent NetKAT
(
Wagemaker et al. 2022
)

use KA to model
programs
,
whereas
in
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
we use the KA structure to describe the
data
that programs exchange,
while the programs themselves are written in a separate language.
We have also taken
inspiration from languages for programming with XML data

(
Hosoya et al. 2005
;
Benzaken et al. 2003
;
Frisch et al. 2002
, etc.)
using types
based on regular expressions.

7.
Conclusions and Future Work

We have proposed a new static type system for stream
programming, motivated by a novel variant of BI logic and
able to capture both complex temporal patterns and deterministic
parallel processing.

In the future, we hope to add more types to
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
. Adding a support
for
b
ags — unbounded parallelism, the parallel analog of Kleene star —
would enable dynamic partitioning.
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
also lacks function types.
The proof theory of BI would imply that there should be two (one for each context former),
but we have yet to investigate what these functions might mean in the streaming setting.

Further theoretical investigations include (1) alternate semantics for stream types, including
a denotational semantics as pomset morphisms, Kahn Process Networks
(
Kahn 1974
)
, or some category of state machines,
(2) eliminating the inertness restriction on let-bindings,
and (3) adding a
guardedness
condition on recursive calls
to ensure termination and hence productivity.

On the applied side, we plan to build a distributed implementation of delta
by compiling
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
terms to programs for an existing stream processing system like
Apache Storm
(
Foundation 2019e
)
, thus inheriting its desirable fault-tolerance and
delivery guarantees. We hope
to build such a compiler and use it as a platform for experimenting with
type-enabled optimizations and resource usage analysis.

Acknowledgements.

We thank the reviewers for their feedback.
We also thank Justin Lubin for feedback on drafts of this paper, and Alex
Kavvos, Andrew Hirsch, Mae Milano, and Michael Arntzenius for helpful
discussions about early versions of this work.

B.1.
Running Fold

We can also define a
running

which outputs its partial results as it goes.

{

>{

(

B.2.
Partitioning

A crucial streaming idiom is partitioning, where a homogeneous stream of
data is split into two or more parallel streams, which are then
routed to different downstream nodes in the dataflow graph.
The purpose of partitioning is to expose parallelism: the different
downstream operators can be run separately, potentially on different physical machines.
Depending on the situation, a programmer may choose to use different partitioning strategies.
In
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
, some common partitioning strategies are implementable as transformers.

Round Robin Partitioning

A round-robin partitioner fairly distributes an incoming stream of type

s^{\star}
into a parallel pair of streams
s^{\star}\|s^{\star}
.
It does this by sending the first element to the left branch, the second to the right, the third to the left, and so on.
In
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
, we write this by maintaining a Boolean accumulator,
and negating after each item. If the Boolean is true, we send the element
left, if it’s false, we send it right.

⬇

fun

roundRobin
[
s
]{
b

:

Bool
}(
xs

:

s
*)

:

s
*

||

s
*

=

case

xs

of

nil

=>

(
nil
,
nil
)

|

y
::
ys

=>

let

(
zs
,
ws
)

=

roundRobin
{!
b
}(
ys
)

in

if

{
b
}

then

(
y
::
zs
,
ws
)

else

(
zs
,
y
::
ws
)

Decision-Based Partitioning

A decision-based partitioner routes stream elements based on the result of a predicate.

⬇

fun

decPartition
[
s
,
t
,
r
]<
f

:

(
s
)

->

t

+

r
>(
xs

:

s
*)

:

t
*

||

r
*

=

case

xs

of

nil

=>

(
nil
,
nil
)

|

y
::
ys

=>

let

(
ts
,
rs
)

=

decPartition
(
ys
)

in

case

f
(
y
)

of

inl

t

=>

(
t
::
ts
,
rs
)

|

inr

r

=>

(
ts
,
r
::
rs
)

B.3.
Windowing and Punctuation

Many kinds of windows have been considered in the literature.
The most common windows are event-based — windows defined by the number of
elements they’ll contain — and time-based — windows which contain
all the events from a fixed length of time. Windows can also be tumbling —
the next window starts after the previous ends — or sliding —
every event could begin a new window.

In
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
, windowed operators are just
map
s over a stream whose
elements are windows. Given a per-window stream transformer
f

which takes windows
s^{\star}
to a result type
t
, and a “windowing strategy”
win

which takes a stream
r^{\star}
and turns it into a stream of windows
\left(s^{\star}\right)^{\star}
,
we can write a windowed operation of type
r^{\star}\to t^{\star}
as follows:

xs : r
⋆
|- map(f)(win(xs)) : t
⋆
.

For example, if we wanted to compute a size-3 sliding sum of a stream of

Int
s, we would use a windower
win
which takes
\texttt{Int}^{\star}

to
\left(\texttt{Int}^{\star}\right)^{\star}
where the inner streams are the windows,
and
f
from
\texttt{Int}^{\star}
to
Int
is the sum operation.

Every per-window function commonly used in stream processing practice operates on entire windows at once,
which is accomplished in
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
by
wait
-ing on
the whole window, and then
aggregating it with an embedded historical program.
For this reason, we focus primarily on the window construction aspect.

Fixed-Size Tumbling Windows

The
k
-size tumbling windower creates windows of size
k
,
where each new window starts immediately after the last window ended.
For instance when
k=2
, a stream
1,2,4,7,3,8,\dots
turns into a stream

\langle 1,2\rangle,\langle 4,7\rangle,\langle 3,8\rangle,\dots
.
The code for a fixed-size tumbling window is exactly the functional code for
computing
k
-strides of a list, by grouping together the first
k
elements,
and recursing down the rest of the stream.

⬇

fun

firstN
[
s
]{
n

:

Int
}(
xs

:

s
*)

:

s
*

.

s
*

=

case

xs

of

nil

=>

(
nil
;
nil
)

|

y
::
ys

=>

if

{
n

>

0}

then

let

(
predN
;
rest
)

=

firstN
{
n
-1}(
ys
)

in

(
y
::
predN
;
rest
)

else

(
nil
;
y
::
ys
)

fun

tumble
[
s
]{
k

:

Int
}(
xs

:

s
*)

:

s
**

=

let

(
first
;
rest
)

=

firstN
[
s
]{
k
}(
xs
)

in

first

::

tumble
{
k
}(
rest
)

k
-size window transformers can actually have the even stronger output
type
\left(s^{k}\right)^{\star}
, where
s^{k}
is the
k
-fold concatenation of
s
. If the window function being used
requires that the windows all have exactly size
k
(like taking pairwise differences for
k=2
),
this type can be used instead. The following program implements size-2 windows with this stronger type
by casing two-deep into the stream at a time, and pairing up elements into concatenation pairs.

⬇

fun

parsepairs
[
s
](
xs

:

s
*)

:

(
s

.

s
)*

=

case

xs

of

nil

=>

nil

|

y

::

ys

=>

case

ys

of

nil

=>

nil

|

z

::

zs

=>

(
y
;
z
)

::

parsepairs
(
zs
)

Fixed-Size Sliding Windows

A
k
-sized sliding windower produces a new window for each
new element, including both the new element and the
k-1
previous ones.
The code for this windower keeps the current window under construction in
memory. When each new stream element arrives, we emit the current window. For
the first
k
elements, we only add to the window. After
k
, we start evicting
from the window.

⬇

fun

slidingWindower
(
acc

:

s
⋆
;

xs

:

s
⋆
)

:

s
⋆⋆

=

case

xs

of

nil

=>

acc

::

nil

|

y
::
ys

=>

wait

y

do

let

next

=

{
if

|
acc
|

<

k

then

y

::

acc

else

y

::

(
init

acc
)}

in

next

::

slidingWindower
(
next
;
xs
)

end

Punctuation-Based Windows

Time-based windows are commonly implemented
by way of
punctuation
: unit elements
inserted into a stream to authoritatively mark
that a period of time has ended.
This is required because in the presence
of network delays, it’s impossible to know
if a time period is over (and so a window can be emitted)
or if there are more elements in the period to arrive.
A punctuated stream has type
(1+s)^{\star}
,
where the punctuation events mark the end of each time period.

The following code computes a windowed stream
\left(s^{\star}\right)^{\star}
from
a punctuated stream
(\varepsilon+s)^{\star}
by emitting windows which are the (potentially empty) runs
of
s
s between punctuation marks.

⬇

fun

tilFirstPunc
[
s
](
xs

:

(
Eps

+

s
)*)

:

s
*

.

(
Eps

+

s
)*

=

case

xs

of

nil

=>

(
nil
;
nil
)

|

y
::
ys

=>

case

y

of

inl

_

=>

(
nil
;
ys
)

|

inr

s

=>

let

(
cur
;
rest
)

=

tilFirstPunc
(
ys
)

in

(
s
::
cur
;
rest
)

fun

puncWindow
[
s
](
xs

:

(
Eps

+

s
)*)

:

s
**

=

let

(
run
;
rest
)

=

tilFirstPunc
[
s
](
xs
)

in

run

::

puncWindow
(
rest
)

Merging Streams and Synchronizing Punctuation

Parallel streams of star type can be
synchronized
, pairing off one element
from one stream with one element of another. Given a stream of type
s^{\star}\|t^{\star}
,
we can produce a stream of type
\left(s\|t\right)^{\star}
.
This type’s similarity to the standard functional program
zip
is more than just surface level:
the program below has essentially the same code.

⬇

fun

sync
[
s
,
t
](
xs

:

s
*,

ys

:

t
*)

:

(
s

||

t
)*

=

case

xs

of

nil

=>

nil

|

x
’::
xs
’

=>

case

ys

of

nil

=>

nil

|

y
’::
ys
’

=>

wait

x
’,
y
’

do

{(
x
’,
y
’)}

::

sync
(
xs
’,
ys
’)

end

Semantically, this program
wait
s until a full element from each of the
parallel input streams has arrived, sends them both out, and then continues
with zipping the two tails. This is necessarily blocking: the output type
guarantees that exactly one
s
and
t
will be produced before the next pair
begins, and so we must wait for both to arrive before sending the other out.
The upshot is that because this program is well typed in
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
, it is
necessarily deterministic. This gives us the deterministic merge operation that
was needed to prevent the bug when averaging data from a pair of sensors in
Section
2
.

Moreover, for parallel streams of windows, synchronization enables
databases-style
streaming joins
. Given parallel
streams
\left(s^{\star}\right)^{\star}
and
\left(t^{\star}\right)^{\star}
, we can
synchronize to get
\left(s^{\star}\|t^{\star}\right)^{\star}
, and then apply a
join operation to each parallel pair of windows.

Appendix C
Technicalities

This appendix collects technical definitions that did not fit in the
main body of the paper.

C.1.
Basics

Stream types are defined by the following grammar. The base types included are the unit type
1
which types streams that contain
exactly one unit element, the type of the empty stream
\varepsilon
, and the type of streams consisting of a single integer,
Int
.
Larger types include the concatenation type
s\cdot t
, the sum type
s+t
, the parallel stream type
s\|t
, and the star type
s^{\star}
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}s,t,r:=&1\mid\varepsilon\mid\texttt{Int}\mid s\cdot t\mid s+t\mid s\|t\mid s^{\star}\end{array}

Contexts in the stream types calculus system have a bunched structure. The context former
\Gamma\,,\Delta

corresponds to the parallel type, while the context former
\Gamma\,;\Delta
corresponds to the concatenation type.
The two context formers share a unit, written as “
\cdot
”.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\Gamma::=&\cdot\mid\Gamma\,,\Gamma\mid\Gamma\,;\Gamma\mid x:s\\
\end{array}

A stream type is
null
if it includes no data.
Null types are parallel combinations of
\varepsilon
s.

Definition C.1 (Nullable).

We define a judgment
s\;\texttt{null}
as follows:

\frac{}{\varepsilon\;\texttt{null}}\par\frac{s\;\texttt{null}\quad t\;\texttt{null}}{s\|t\;\texttt{null}}

We extend to contexts pointwise.

\frac{}{\cdot\;\texttt{null}}\par\frac{s\;\texttt{null}}{x:s\;\texttt{null}}\par\frac{\Gamma\;\texttt{null}\quad\Gamma^{\prime}\;\texttt{null}}{\Gamma\,,\Gamma^{\prime}\;\texttt{null}}\par\frac{\Gamma\;\texttt{null}\quad\Gamma^{\prime}\;\texttt{null}}{\Gamma\,;\Gamma^{\prime}\;\texttt{null}}

Prefixes are also like in the main paper,
with a definition
p\;\texttt{maximal}
for “complete” prefixes, and a typing relation
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
.

Definition C.2 (Prefixes).

The grammar of prefixes is given by:

\begin{split}p::=\texttt{oneEmp}\ |\ \texttt{oneFull}\ |\ \texttt{epsEmp}\ |\ \texttt{parPair}(p,p^{\prime})\\
\ |\ \texttt{catFst}(p)\ |\ \texttt{catBoth}(p,p^{\prime})\\
\ |\ \texttt{sumEmp}\ |\ \texttt{sumInl}(p)\ |\ \texttt{sumInr}(p)\\
\ |\ \texttt{starEmp}\ |\ \texttt{starDone}\\
\ |\ \texttt{starFirst}(p)\ |\ \texttt{starRest}(p,p^{\prime})\end{split}

Definition C.3 (Maximal Prefix).

\frac{}{\texttt{epsEmp}\;\texttt{maximal}}\par\frac{}{\texttt{oneFull}\;\texttt{maximal}}\par\frac{p_{1}\;\texttt{maximal}\quad p_{2}\;\texttt{maximal}}{\texttt{parPair}(p_{1},p_{2})\;\texttt{maximal}}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p_{1}\;\texttt{maximal}\quad p_{2}\;\texttt{maximal}}{\texttt{catBoth}(p_{1},p_{2})\;\texttt{maximal}}\par\frac{p\;\texttt{maximal}}{\texttt{sumInl}(p)\;\texttt{maximal}}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p\;\texttt{maximal}}{\texttt{sumInr}(p)\;\texttt{maximal}}\par\frac{}{\texttt{starDone}\;\texttt{maximal}}\par\frac{p\;\texttt{maximal}\quad p^{\prime}\;\texttt{maximal}}{\texttt{starRest}(p,p^{\prime})\;\texttt{maximal}}

Definition C.4 (Well-Typed Prefixes).

\frac{}{\texttt{epsEmp}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(\varepsilon\right)}\par\frac{}{\texttt{oneEmp}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(1\right)}\par\frac{}{\texttt{oneFull}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(1\right)}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p_{1}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)\quad p_{2}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(t\right)\quad}{\texttt{parPair}(p_{1},p_{2})\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\|t\right)}\par\frac{p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)}{\texttt{catFst}(p)\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\cdot t\right)}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p_{1}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)\quad p_{1}\;\texttt{maximal}\quad p_{2}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(t\right)}{\texttt{catBoth}(p_{1},p_{2})\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\cdot t\right)}\par\frac{}{\texttt{sumEmp}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s+t\right)}\par\frac{p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)}{\texttt{sumInl}(p)\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s+t\right)}\par\frac{p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(t\right)}{\texttt{sumInr}(p)\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s+t\right)}\par\frac{}{\texttt{starEmp}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s^{\star}\right)}\par\frac{}{\texttt{starDone}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s^{\star}\right)}\par\frac{p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)}{\texttt{starFirst}(p)\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s^{\star}\right)}\par\frac{p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)\quad p\;\texttt{maximal}\quad p^{\prime}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s^{\star}\right)}{\texttt{starRest}(p,p^{\prime})\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s^{\star}\right)}

For each type
s
, we define the “empty” prefix
\texttt{emp}_{s}
inductively on the structure of
s
.

Definition C.5 (Empty Prefix).

The empty prefix is defined as follows:

(
\varepsilon
)

\texttt{emp}_{\varepsilon}=\texttt{epsEmp}

(
1
)

\texttt{emp}_{1}=\texttt{oneEmp}

(
s\|t
)

\texttt{emp}_{s\|t}=\texttt{parPair}(\texttt{emp}_{s},\texttt{emp}_{t})

(
s+t
)

\texttt{emp}_{s+t}=\texttt{sumEmp}

(
s\cdot t
)

\texttt{emp}_{s\cdot t}=\texttt{catFst}(\texttt{emp}_{s})

(
s^{\star}
)

\texttt{emp}_{s^{\star}}=\texttt{starEmp}

We lift this to contexts in the natural way,
with
\texttt{emp}_{\cdot}=\texttt{epsEmp}
, and

\texttt{emp}_{\Gamma\,;\Delta}=\texttt{catFst}(\texttt{emp}_{\Gamma})
, and

\texttt{emp}_{\Gamma\,,\Delta}=\texttt{parPair}(\texttt{emp}_{\Gamma},\texttt{emp}_{\Delta})
.

Theorem C.6 (Empty Prefix is Well-Typed).

\texttt{emp}_{s}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)

Definition C.7 (Prefix is Empty).

\par\frac{}{\texttt{epsEmp}\;\texttt{empty}}\par\frac{}{\texttt{oneEmp}\;\texttt{empty}}\par\frac{p_{1}\;\texttt{empty}\quad p_{2}\;\texttt{empty}}{\texttt{parPair}(p_{1},p_{2})\;\texttt{empty}}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p\;\texttt{empty}}{\texttt{catFst}(p)\;\texttt{empty}}\par\frac{}{\texttt{sumEmp}\;\texttt{empty}}\par\frac{}{\texttt{starEmp}\;\texttt{empty}}

Theorem C.8 (Empty Prefix Is Empty).

\texttt{emp}_{s}\;\texttt{empty}

Proof.

Induction on
s
.
∎

Theorem C.9 (Empty And Maximal Means Nullable).

If
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
, and simultaneously
p\;\texttt{empty}
and
p\;\texttt{maximal}
, then
s\;\texttt{null}
.

Proof.

By induction on
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)

∎

C.2.
Derivatives

We define a 3-place relation
\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\sim s^{\prime}
between a prefix and two types.

Definition C.10 (Derivatives).

\frac{}{\delta_{\texttt{epsEmp}}\left(\varepsilon\right)\sim\varepsilon}\par\frac{}{\delta_{\texttt{oneEmp}}\left(1\right)\sim 1}\par\frac{}{\delta_{\texttt{oneFull}}\left(1\right)\sim\varepsilon}\par\frac{\delta_{p_{1}}\left(s\right)\sim s^{\prime}\quad\delta_{p_{2}}\left(t\right)\sim t^{\prime}}{\delta_{\texttt{parPair}(p_{1},p_{2})}\left(s\|t\right)\sim s^{\prime}\|t^{\prime}}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\sim s^{\prime}}{\delta_{\texttt{catFst}(p)}\left(s\cdot t\right)\sim s^{\prime}\cdot t}\par\frac{\delta_{p_{2}}\left(t\right)\sim t^{\prime}}{\delta_{\texttt{catBoth}(p_{1},p_{2})}\left(s\cdot t\right)\sim t^{\prime}}\par\frac{}{\delta_{\texttt{sumEmp}}\left(s+t\right)\sim s+t}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\sim s^{\prime}}{\delta_{\texttt{sumInl}(p)}\left(s+t\right)\sim s^{\prime}}\par\frac{\delta_{p}\left(t\right)\sim t^{\prime}}{\delta_{\texttt{sumInl}(p)}\left(s+t\right)\sim t^{\prime}}\par\frac{}{\delta_{\texttt{starEmp}}\left(s^{\star}\right)\sim s^{\star}}\par\frac{}{\delta_{\texttt{starDone}}\left(s^{\star}\right)\sim\varepsilon}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\sim s^{\prime}}{\delta_{\texttt{starFirst}(p)}\left(s^{\star}\right)\sim s^{\prime}\cdot s^{\star}}\par\frac{\delta_{p^{\prime}}\left(s^{\star}\right)\sim s^{\prime}}{\delta_{\texttt{starRest}(p,p^{\prime})}\left(s^{\star}\right)\sim s^{\prime}}\par

Definition C.11 (Context Derivatives).

\frac{}{\delta_{\eta}\left(\cdot\right)\sim\cdot}\par\frac{\eta(x)\mapsto p\quad\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\sim s^{\prime}}{\delta_{\eta}\left(x:s\right)\sim x:s^{\prime}}\par\frac{\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}\quad\delta_{\eta}\left(\Delta\right)\sim\Delta^{\prime}}{\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma\,,\Delta\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}\,,\Delta^{\prime}}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}\quad\delta_{\eta}\left(\Delta\right)\sim\Delta^{\prime}}{\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma\,;\Delta\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}\,;\Delta^{\prime}}

Derivatives are functions defined when the prefix input is well-typed.

Theorem C.12 (Derivative Function).

For any
p
and
s
, there is at most one
s^{\prime}
such that
\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\sim s^{\prime}
.
If
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
, then such an
s^{\prime}
exists.

Proof.

Induction on the derivation of
\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\sim s^{\prime}
for uniqueness, and
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
for existence.
∎

When it’s guaranteed to exist, we write this
s^{\prime}
simply as
\delta_{p}\left(s\right)
.
The empty prefix is the identity for the derivative operator.

Theorem C.13 (Empty Prefix Derivative).

\delta_{\texttt{emp}_{s}}\left(s\right)=s
.

Theorem C.14 (Empty Context Derivative).

If
\eta\,\texttt{emptyOn}\,\Gamma
and
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
then

\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma\right)=\Gamma
.

Theorem C.15 (Context Derivatives Function).

There is at most one
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}
,
and the
\Gamma^{\prime}
exists when
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
.

Proof.

Uniqueness by induction on the derivation of
\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}
,
existence by induction on the derivation of
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
.
∎

Theorem C.16 (Maximal Derivative iff Nullable).

If
\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\sim s^{\prime}
then
p\;\texttt{maximal}
if and only if
s^{\prime}\;\texttt{null}

Theorem C.17 (Only Prefix of a Null Type is Empty).

If
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
and
s\;\texttt{null}
, then
p=\texttt{emp}_{s}

C.3.
Environments

Definition C.18 (Environments and Typing).

An environment is a partial map
\eta:\texttt{Var}\to\texttt{Prefix}
.
We write
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
to mean that
\eta
is a well-typed environment for
\Gamma
.

\frac{}{\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\cdot\right)}\par\frac{\eta(x)\mapsto p\quad p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)}{\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(x:s\right)}\par\frac{\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)\quad\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Delta\right)\quad}{\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\,,\Delta\right)}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)\quad\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Delta\right)\quad\eta\,\texttt{emptyOn}\,\Delta\vee\eta\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,\Gamma}{\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\,;\Delta\right)}

Definition C.19 (All Maximal, All Empty, Agreement).

For a set
S
, we say
\eta\,\texttt{emptyOn}\,S
if for all
x\in S
, there is some
p
such that
\eta(x)\mapsto p
, and
p\;\texttt{empty}
.
We say
\eta\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,S
if for all
x\in S
, there is some
p
such that
\eta(x)\mapsto p
, and
p\;\texttt{maximal}
.
We write
\eta\,\texttt{emptyOn}\,\Gamma
and
\eta\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,\Gamma
to mean
\eta\,\texttt{emptyOn}\,\text{Dom}\left(\Gamma\right)
and
\eta\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,\text{Dom}\left(\Gamma\right)
, respectively.
We also write
\eta\,\texttt{emptyOn}\,e
and
\eta\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,e
to mean
\eta\,\texttt{emptyOn}\,\text{fv}(e)
and
\eta\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,\text{fv}(e)
, respectively.

Finally, we say that
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
agree on
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
, written
\texttt{agree}\left(\eta,\eta^{\prime},\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\right)
if

\eta\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,\Delta\implies\eta^{\prime}\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,\Delta^{\prime}
, and
\eta\,\texttt{emptyOn}\,\Delta\implies\eta^{\prime}\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,\Delta^{\prime}

An environment is also an environment for every subcontext.

Theorem C.20 (Environment Subcontext Lookup).

If
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(\Delta)\right)
, then
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Delta\right)

Proof.

Induction on
\Gamma(-)
.
∎

Moreover, replacing a the environment
\eta|_{\Delta}
for a subcontext
\Delta
with another environment
\eta^{\prime}
for another context
\Delta^{\prime}

yields a well-typed context, so long as
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
agree on
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
. If
\eta
was maximal (on
\Delta
) then
\eta^{\prime}
must also be
(on
\Delta^{\prime}
), and if
\eta
was empty (on
\Delta
), then
\eta^{\prime}
must also be empty (on
\Delta^{\prime}
).

Theorem C.21 (Environment Subcontext Bind).

If
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(\Delta)\right)
and
\eta^{\prime}\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Delta^{\prime}\right)

such that
\texttt{agree}\left(\eta,\eta^{\prime},\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\right)

then
\eta\cdot\eta^{\prime}\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(\Delta^{\prime})\right)

Proof.

Induction on the structure of
\Gamma(-)
, inverting everything in sight.
∎

Theorem C.22 (Environment Par Bind).

If
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(z:s\|t)\right)
and
\eta(z)\mapsto\texttt{parPair}(p_{1},p_{2})

then
\eta[x\mapsto p_{1},y\mapsto p_{2}]\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(x:s\,,y:t)\right)

Proof.

By Theorem
C.21
.
∎

Theorem C.23 (Environment Cat Bind 1).

If
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(z:s\cdot t)\right)
and
\eta(z)\mapsto\texttt{catFst}(p)

then
\eta[x\mapsto p,y\mapsto\texttt{emp}_{t}]\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(x:s\,;y:t)\right)

Proof.

By Theorem
C.21
.
∎

Theorem C.24 (Environment Cat Bind 2).

If
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(z:s\cdot t)\right)
and
\eta(z)\mapsto\texttt{catBoth}(p_{1},p_{2})

then
\eta[x\mapsto p_{1},y\mapsto p_{2}]\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(x:s\,;y:t)\right)

Proof.

By Theorem
C.21
.
∎

Lastly, the structure of the above subcontext replacement operation is compatible with derivatives.
Taking the derivative of
\Gamma(\Delta)
by
\eta
yields
\Gamma^{\prime}(\delta_{\eta}\left(\Delta\right))
for some
\Gamma^{\prime}(-)
,
and for
any
other filler
\Delta_{0}
and environment
\eta_{0}\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Delta_{0}\right)
,
the outer derivative bit of the derivative remains unchanged:
\delta_{\eta\cup\eta_{0}}\left(\Gamma(\Delta_{0})\right)
is
\Gamma^{\prime}(\delta_{\eta_{0}}\left(\Delta_{0}\right))

Theorem C.25 (Environment Subcontext Bind Derivative).

If
\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma(\Delta)\right)\sim\Gamma_{0}
then there is some
\Gamma^{\prime}(-)
such that
for all
\Delta^{\prime}
and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
and
\eta^{\prime}
, if
\delta_{\eta^{\prime}}\left(\Delta^{\prime}\right)\sim\Delta^{\prime\prime}
and
\texttt{agree}\left(\eta,\eta^{\prime},\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\right)

then
\delta_{\eta\cup\eta^{\prime}}\left(\Gamma(\Delta^{\prime})\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}(\Delta^{\prime\prime})

Proof.

Induction on
\Gamma(-)
.
∎

Theorem C.26 (Environment Par Derivative).

If
\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma(z:s\|t)\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}(z:s^{\prime}\|t^{\prime})

and
\eta(z)\mapsto\texttt{parPair}(p_{1},p_{2})

then
\delta_{\eta[x\mapsto p_{1},y\mapsto p_{2}]}\left(\Gamma(x:s\,,y:t)\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}(x:s^{\prime}\,,y:t^{\prime})

Proof.

By Theorem
C.25
.
∎

Theorem C.27 (Environment Cat Derivative 1).

If
\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma(z:s\cdot t)\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}(z:s^{\prime}\cdot t)

and
\eta(z)\mapsto\texttt{catFst}(p)

then
\delta_{\eta[x\mapsto p,y\mapsto\texttt{emp}_{t}]}\left(\Gamma(x:s\,;y:t)\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}(x:s^{\prime}\,;y:t)

Proof.

By Theorem
C.25
.
∎

Theorem C.28 (Environment Cat Derivative 2).

If
\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma(z:s\cdot t)\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}(z:t^{\prime})

and
\eta(z)\mapsto\texttt{catBoth}(p_{1},p_{2})

then
\delta_{\eta[x\mapsto p_{1},y\mapsto p_{2}]}\left(\Gamma(x:s\,;y:t)\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}(x:s^{\prime}\,;y:t^{\prime})

Proof.

By Theorem
C.25
.
∎

Theorem C.29 (Environment Lookup).

For any
\eta
, there is at most one
p
so that
\eta(x)\mapsto p
.
When
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(x:s)\right)
, this
p
exists, and
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
.

Proof.

The “at most one”
p
is immediate from the fact that
\eta
is a deterministic partial function.
If
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(x:s)\right)
then
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(x:s\right)
by Theorem
C.20
.
By inversion, there is some
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
such that
\eta(x)\mapsto p
.
∎

Theorem C.30 (Environment Lookup Derivative).

Suppose:

(1)

\eta(x)=p

(2)

\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(x:s)\right)

(3)

\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\sim s^{\prime}

(4)

\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma(x:s)\right)\sim\Gamma_{0}

Then there is some
\Gamma^{\prime}(-)
such that
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma^{\prime}(x:s^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Immediate by Theorem
C.25

∎

C.4.
Concatenation

More generally, we often want to concatenate a prefix
p
of
s

with a prefix
p^{\prime}
of
\delta_{p}\left(s\right)
. This is defined with another 3-place,
type-indexed relation.

Definition C.31 (Prefix Concatenation).

We define a relation
p\cdot p^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}
.

\frac{}{\texttt{epsEmp}\cdot\texttt{epsEmp}\sim\texttt{epsEmp}}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(1\right)}{\texttt{oneEmp}\cdot p\sim p}\par\frac{}{\texttt{oneFull}\cdot\texttt{epsEmp}\sim\texttt{oneFull}}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p_{1}\cdot p_{1}^{\prime}\sim p_{1}^{\prime\prime}\quad p_{2}\cdot p_{2}^{\prime}\sim p_{2}^{\prime\prime}\quad}{\texttt{parPair}(p_{1},p_{2})\cdot\texttt{parPair}(p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime})\sim\texttt{parPair}(p_{1}^{\prime\prime},p_{2}^{\prime\prime})}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p\cdot p^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}}{\texttt{catFst}(p)\cdot\texttt{catFst}(p^{\prime})\sim\texttt{catFst}(p^{\prime\prime})}\par\frac{p\cdot p^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}}{\texttt{catFst}(p)\cdot\texttt{catBoth}(p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime\prime})\sim\texttt{catBoth}(p^{\prime\prime},p^{\prime\prime\prime})}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\texttt{catBoth}(p,p^{\prime})\cdot p^{\prime\prime}\sim\texttt{catBoth}(p,p^{\prime\prime\prime})}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{}{\texttt{sumEmp}\cdot p\sim p}\par\frac{p^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}}{\texttt{sumInl}(p)\cdot p^{\prime}\sim\texttt{sumInl}(p^{\prime\prime})}\par\frac{p\cdot p^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}}{\texttt{sumInr}(p)\cdot p^{\prime}\sim\texttt{sumInr}(p^{\prime\prime})}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{}{\texttt{starEmp}\cdot p\sim p}\par\frac{}{\texttt{starDone}\cdot\texttt{epsEmp}\sim\texttt{starDone}}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p\cdot p^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}}{\texttt{starFirst}(p)\cdot\texttt{catFst}(p^{\prime})\sim\texttt{starFirst}(p^{\prime\prime})}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p\cdot p^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}}{\texttt{starFirst}(p)\cdot\texttt{catBoth}(p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime\prime})\sim\texttt{starRest}(p^{\prime\prime},p^{\prime\prime\prime})}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{p^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\texttt{starRest}(p,p^{\prime})\cdot p^{\prime\prime}\sim\texttt{starRest}(p,p^{\prime\prime\prime})}\par

This relation is a function when the inputs are well-typed.
Because of this, when
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
and
p^{\prime}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\right)
,
we write
p\cdot p^{\prime}
for the unique
p^{\prime\prime}
that the following theorem guarantees.

Theorem C.32 (Prefix Concatenation Function).

For all
p,p^{\prime}
and
s
, there is at most one
p^{\prime\prime}
such that
p\cdot p^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}
.
If
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
and
p^{\prime}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\right)
, then
such a
p^{\prime\prime}
exists, and satisfies:

(1)

p^{\prime\prime}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)

(2)

\delta_{p^{\prime\prime}}\left(s\right)=\delta_{p^{\prime}}\left(\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\right)

Proof.

Existence, (1), and (2) follow by induction on the derivation of
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
. Uniqueness is immediate by the fact that the relation is a function.
∎

Theorem C.33 (Prefix Concatenation Empty).

If
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
, then
\texttt{emp}_{s}\cdot p\sim p
and
p\cdot\texttt{emp}_{\delta_{p}\left(s\right)}\sim p

Proof.

Induction on the derivation of
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
.
∎

Theorem C.34 (Maximal Prefix Concatenation).

Suppose
p\cdot p^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}
.
If
p^{\prime\prime}
is maximal, then
p^{\prime}
is maximal. If
p
or
p^{\prime}
is maximal, then
p^{\prime\prime}
is maximal.
Moreover, if
p
is maximal, then
p^{\prime\prime}=p
.

Proof.

Induction on the derivation of
p\cdot p^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

Theorem C.35 (Prefix Concatenation Associativity).

p\cdot\left(p^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime\prime}\right)=\left(p\cdot p^{\prime}\right)\cdot p^{\prime\prime}
,
when defined.

Proof.

Induction on derivations of concatenation.
∎

Definition C.36 (Environment Concatenation).

We write
\eta\cdot\eta^{\prime}\sim\eta^{\prime\prime}
to mean that

\eta^{\prime\prime}
is the function defined on the largest subset
S
of
\text{Dom}\left(\eta\right)\cap\text{Dom}\left(\eta^{\prime}\right)

such that for all
x\in S
, the prefix concatenation
\eta(x)\cdot\eta^{\prime}(x)\sim p
exists, and
\eta^{\prime\prime}(x)=p
, for all
x\in S
.

Theorem C.37 (Environment Concatenation Function).

For any
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
, there is at most one
\eta^{\prime\prime}
such that
\eta\cdot\eta^{\prime}\sim\eta^{\prime\prime}
,
and such an
\eta^{\prime\prime}
exists when
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
and
\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\eta^{\prime}\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(\Gamma^{\prime}\right)
.

Proof.

Uniqueness by the "greatest" property, existence by Theorem
C.32
.
∎

Theorem C.38 (Environment Concatenation Correctness).

If
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
and
\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\eta^{\prime}\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma^{\prime}\right)
, and
\eta\cdot\eta^{\prime}\sim\eta^{\prime\prime}
,
then
\eta^{\prime\prime}\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
, and if
\delta_{\eta^{\prime}}\left(\Gamma^{\prime}\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
then
\delta_{\eta^{\prime\prime}}\left(\Gamma\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
.

Theorem C.39 (Environment Concatenation Empty).

If
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
and
\eta\cdot\eta^{\prime}\sim\eta^{\prime\prime}
, then:

•

If
\eta\,\texttt{emptyOn}\,S
and then
\eta^{\prime\prime}|_{S}=\eta^{\prime}|_{S}

•

If
\eta^{\prime}\,\texttt{emptyOn}\,S
, then
\eta^{\prime\prime}|_{S}=\eta|_{S}

Proof.

Induction on the derivation of
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
, using Theorem
C.33
.
∎

Theorem C.40 (Maximal Environment Concatenation).

If
\eta\cdot\eta^{\prime}\sim\eta^{\prime\prime}
, then
\eta\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,S
or
\eta^{\prime}\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,S
if and only if
\eta^{\prime\prime}\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,S
.

Proof.

Immediate corollary of Theorem
C.34

∎

Theorem C.41 (Prefix Concatenation Associativity).

\eta\cdot\left(\eta^{\prime}\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime}\right)=\left(\eta\cdot\eta^{\prime}\right)\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime}
,
when defined.

Proof.

Corollary of Theorem
C.35

∎

C.5.
Historical Contexts

Definition C.42 (Historical Context).

Contexts
\Omega:=\cdot\,|\,\Omega,x:A
are fully structural contexts, where the
A
are STLC types.

A stream type is “flattened” into an STLC type by turning concatenations and parallels
into products, and stars into lists.

Definition C.43 (Type and Context Flatten).

For
s
a stream type, we define its flattening into an STLC type, denoted
\langle s\rangle
, inductively:

•

\langle 1\rangle=1

•

\langle\varepsilon\rangle=1

•

\langle s\cdot t\rangle=\langle s\rangle\times\langle t\rangle

•

\langle s\|t\rangle=\langle s\rangle\times\langle t\rangle

•

\langle s+t\rangle=\langle s\rangle+\langle t\rangle

•

\langle s^{\star}\rangle=\texttt{list}\left(\langle s\rangle\right)

For
\Gamma
a bunched context, we define its flattening to a standard context,
\langle\Gamma\rangle
inductively:

•

\langle\cdot\rangle=\cdot

•

\langle x:s\rangle=x:\langle s\rangle

•

\langle\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\Gamma\rangle,\langle\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle

•

\langle\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\Gamma\rangle,\langle\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle

For an STLC value
v:\langle s\rangle
, we write
\texttt{toPrefix}_{s}\left(v\right)
for the maximal prefix of type
s

that it corresponds to. Dually, for a maximal prefix
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
, we write
\langle p\rangle
for the STLC value of type

\langle s\rangle
it corresponds to.

Definition C.44 (Historical Programs and Substitutions).

Fix a language of terms
M
, with type system
\Omega\vdash M:A
. Write its semantics as
M\downarrow v
.
We assume that this relation is a decidable partial function, in the sense that
M
evaluates to at most one
v
, and it is
decidable whether or not such a
v
exists. We write substitutions
\theta:\Omega^{\prime}\to\Omega
. Substitutions have a contravariant action
on terms, written
M[\theta]
: if
\Omega\vdash M:A
, then
\Omega^{\prime}\vdash M[\theta]:A
.
We lift this substitution action to
\lambda^{\text{ST}}
terms compositionally, substituting into all historical terms.
We write a list of such terms as
\overline{M}
, and lift the typing relation and semantics to
lists of terms, written
\Omega\vdash\overline{M}:\overline{A}
and
\overline{M}\downarrow\theta
.

C.6.
Context Subtyping

The following is a full listing of subtyping rules.

Definition C.45 (Subtyping).

\frac{\Delta\,<:\,\Delta^{\prime}}{\Gamma(\Delta)\,<:\,\Gamma(\Delta^{\prime})}\quad\textsc{Sub-Cong}\par\frac{}{\Gamma\,<:\,\Gamma}\quad\textsc{Sub-Refl}\par\frac{}{\Gamma\,,\Delta\,<:\,\Delta\,,\Gamma}\quad\textsc{Sub-Comma-Exc}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{}{x:s\,<:\,\cdot}\quad\textsc{Sub-Sng-Wkn}\par\par\par\frac{}{\Gamma\,,\Delta\,<:\,\Gamma}\quad\textsc{Sub-Comma-Wkn}\par\frac{}{\Gamma\,;\Delta\,<:\,\Gamma}\quad\textsc{Sub-Semic-Wkn-1}\par\frac{}{\Gamma\,;\Delta\,<:\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Sub-Semic-Wkn-2}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{}{\Gamma\,<:\,\Gamma\,,\cdot}\quad\textsc{Sub-Comma-Unit}\par\frac{}{\Gamma\,<:\,\Gamma\,;\cdot}\quad\textsc{Sub-Semic-Unit-1}\par\frac{}{\Gamma\,<:\,\cdot\,;\Gamma}\quad\textsc{Sub-Semic-Unit-2}

Environment typing is preserved by subtyping, and derivatives preserve subtyping relations between contexts.

Theorem C.46 (Subtyping Preserves Environments).

If
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
and
\Gamma\,<:\,\Delta
then
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Delta\right)

Proof.

By induction on
\Gamma\,<:\,\Delta
, and inversion on
\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
.
∎

Theorem C.47 (Derivatives Preserve Subtyping).

Suppose
\Gamma\,<:\,\Delta
and
\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\delta_{\eta}\left(\Delta\right)\sim\Delta^{\prime}
.
Then,
\Gamma^{\prime}\,<:\,\Delta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By cases on
\Gamma\,<:\,\Delta
, inverting the derivations of

\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}
and

\delta_{\eta}\left(\Delta\right)\sim\Delta^{\prime}
, and using the determinism of the derivative relation.
∎

C.7.
Type System

C.7.1.
Inertness

Most terms, like variables or case expressions, require some non-empty amount of
input to arrive for them to produce a non-empty output. However, this is not
true of all terms: constants like
()
and
nil
, (some) sequential terms
like
\left(\texttt{()};e\right)
and
\texttt{sink}\,\texttt{::}\,e
, and sum terms
\texttt{inl}(e)

produce nonempty output even when given an entirely empty input prefix. Terms
like these contain “information” that they are always ready to produce, even
if there is no input to drive them forward. We call terms that are always ready to produce
output
jumpy
, and terms that are not
inert
.

As described in Section
4.3
, the type system requires that
let-bound terms are always inert to guarantee soundness of the semantics.
In particular, inertness is what guarantees that the “agreement” (Definition
C.19
) requirement
in Theorem
C.21
to hold in the soundness case for
T-Let
.
For arbitrary terms, the maximality component of agreement always holds (this by Lemma
C.56
),
but the emptiness component of agreement requires inertness.

To enforce that the bodies of let-bindings are inert, we track a syntactic over-approximation of inertness with the type system, essentially as an

effect
. This is accomplished by giving every typing judgment an

inertness annotation
,
i::=\texttt{I}\mid\texttt{J}
, and we ensure that if
e
is
typed with annotation
Inert
, then
e
produces empty output when
given an empty input. This invariant is proved as an additional consequence to
the soundness theorem.

We note that the choice to include inertness in the type
system itself, as opposed to a predicate on (typed) terms, is essentially an
arbitrary one: we choose the former to minimize the number of assumptions
running around in our proofs.

For the most part, the inertness analysis is straightforward. Constants like

()
and
nil
, and injections like
\texttt{inl}\left(e\right)
,
\texttt{inr}\left(e\right)
, and

e_{1}\,\texttt{::}\,e_{2}
(secretly the right injection into
\varepsilon+s\cdot s^{\star}
)
all have annotation
J
. Non-buffering elimination forms have the same
inertness as their bodies, and variables and
sink
are inert. The most
important ones are in the rules
T-Cat-R
and
T-Plus-L
(and
the similar ones in
T-Star-L
and
T-Plus-L
).

The inertness requirement for
T-Cat-R
says that if the resulting term

\left(e_{1};e_{2}\right)
is to be typed as inert,
e_{1}
must be inert, and the type of
e_{1}

must not be
null
. Otherwise,
\left(e_{1};e_{2}\right)
could produce a maximal .

The rule for
T-Plus-L
says that it is inert when the buffer
environment does not yet include a decision for which way to go (
\eta(z)=\texttt{sumEmp}
). Note that in practice, this is always satisfied. At the beginning of
execution,
\eta
maps all variables to empty prefixes, and as soon as
\eta(z)

gets either
\texttt{sumInl}(p)
or
\texttt{sumInr}(p)
, we step to the corresponding branch. In
fact, the result of
every
step is inert: otherwise we would’ve output a
larger prefix in that step!

Definition C.48 (Typing Rules).

Figure
7
and Figure
8
present
the full typing rules.

Definition C.49 (Recursion Signature).

A recursion signature
\Sigma
is either empty (signaling that typechecking is not in the body of a recursive function),
or the signature
\Omega\mid\Gamma\to s\,@\,i
of a sequent which defines the recursive function we are currently checking the body of.

\Sigma::=\emptyset\mid\left(\Omega\mid\Gamma\to s\,@\,i\right)

\frac{}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{sink}:\varepsilon\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Eps-R}\par\frac{}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{()}:1\,@\,\texttt{J}}\quad\textsc{T-One-R}\par\frac{}{\Omega\mid\Gamma(x:s)\vdash_{\Sigma}x:s\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Var}\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}e:s\,@\,i\quad\Gamma\,<:\,\Delta}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}e:s\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Sub}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{1}:s\,@\,i\quad\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{2}:t\,@\,i}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}\left(e_{1},e_{2}\right):s\|t\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Par-R}\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{1}:s\,@\,i_{1}\quad\Omega\mid\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{2}:t\,@\,i_{2}\quad i_{3}=\texttt{I}\implies i_{1}=\texttt{I}\wedge\neg\left(s\;\texttt{null}\right)}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\,;\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}\left(e_{1};e_{2}\right):s\cdot t\,@\,i_{3}}\quad\textsc{T-Cat-R}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma(x:s\,,y:t)\vdash_{\Sigma}e:r\,@\,i}{\Omega\mid\Gamma(z:s\|t)\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{let}\,\left(x,y\right)\,=\,z\,\texttt{in}\,e:r\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Par-L}\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma(x:s\,;y:t)\vdash_{\Sigma}e:r\,@\,i}{\Omega\mid\Gamma(z:s\cdot t)\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{let}_{t}\,\left(x;y\right)\,=\,z\,\texttt{in}\,e:r\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Cat-L}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}e:s\,@\,i}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{inl}(e):s+t\,@\,\texttt{J}}\quad\textsc{T-Plus-R-1}\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}e:t\,@\,i}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{inr}(e):s+t\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Plus-R-2}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(z:s+t)\right)\quad\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma(z:s+t)\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}\quad\Omega\mid\Gamma(x:s)\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{1}:r\,@\,i_{1}\quad\Omega\mid\Gamma(y:t)\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{2}:r\,@\,i_{2}\quad i=\texttt{I}\implies\eta(z)=\texttt{sumEmp}}{\Omega\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{case}_{r}\left(\eta;z,x.e_{1},y.e_{2}\right):r\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Plus-L}\par\par

Figure 7.
Full Typing Rules (Part 1)

\frac{}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{nil}:s^{\star}\,@\,\texttt{J}}\quad\textsc{T-Star-R-1}\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{1}:s\,@\,i_{1}\quad\Omega\mid\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{2}:s^{\star}\,@\,i_{2}}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\,;\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{1}\,\texttt{::}\,e_{2}:s^{\star}\,@\,\texttt{J}}\quad\textsc{T-Star-R-2}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(z:s^{\star})\right)\quad\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma(z:s^{\star})\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}\quad\Omega\mid\Gamma(\cdot)\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{1}:r\,@\,i_{1}\quad\Omega\mid\Gamma(x:s;xs:s^{\star})\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{2}:r\,@\,i_{2}\quad i=\texttt{I}\implies\eta(z)=\texttt{starEmp}}{\Omega\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{case}_{s,r}\left(\eta;z,e_{1},x.xs.e_{2}\right):r\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Star-L}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\Omega\vdash M:\langle s\rangle}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}\langle M:s\rangle:s\,@\,\texttt{J}}\quad\textsc{T-HistPgm}\par\frac{\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(x:s)\right)\quad\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma(x:s)\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}\quad\Omega,x:\langle s\rangle\mid\Gamma(\cdot)\vdash_{\Sigma}e:t\,@\,i\quad i^{\prime}=\texttt{I}\implies\neg\left(\eta(z)\;\texttt{maximal}\right)\wedge\neg\left(s\;\texttt{null}\right)}{\Omega\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{wait}_{\eta,t}(x)\left(e\right):t\,@\,i^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{T-Wait}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Delta\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{1}:s\,@\,\texttt{I}\quad\Omega\mid\Gamma(x:s)\vdash_{\Sigma}e_{2}:t\,@\,i}{\Omega\mid\Gamma(\Delta)\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{let}\,x=e_{1}\,\texttt{in}\,e_{2}:t\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Let}\par\frac{\Omega^{\prime}\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Omega\mid\Gamma\to s\,@\,i}A:\Gamma\,@\,i\quad\Omega^{\prime}\vdash\overline{M}:\Omega}{\Omega^{\prime}\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Omega\mid\Gamma\to s\,@\,i}\texttt{rec}\left\{\overline{M}\right\}\left(A\right):s\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Rec}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Omega\mid\Gamma\to s\,@\,i}e:s\,@\,i\quad\Omega^{\prime}\vdash\overline{M}:\Omega\quad\Omega^{\prime}\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}A:\Gamma\,@\,i}{\Omega^{\prime}\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{fix}\left\{\overline{M}\right\}\left(A\right).\left(e\right):s\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Fix}\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}A:\Gamma\,@\,i\quad\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}e:s\,@\,i}{\Omega\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{let}\,\Gamma=A\,\texttt{in}\,e:s\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-ArgsLet}

Figure 8.
Full Typing Rules (Part 2)

These typing rules are mutually defined with another typing judgment

\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}A:\Gamma^{\prime}\,@\,i
, meaning that
A

is a well-typed set of arguments (hence
A
) for a recursive call to a function accepting
inputs
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Here,
A
is an
tree
of terms, with either
comma or semicolon nodes. This judgment ensures that
e_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
has
well-typed bindings for every variable
x:s
in
\Gamma^{\prime}
, and that the
variables that
e_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
uses are used in accordance with
\Gamma
, its
context.

Definition C.50 (Recursive Argument Typing).

A::=\cdot\mid e\mid\left(A,A^{\prime}\right)\mid\left(A;A^{\prime}\right)\mid\left(\cdot;A\right)

\frac{}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}\cdot:\cdot\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Args-Emp}\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}e:s\,@\,i}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}e:\left(x:s\right)\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Args-Sng}\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}A:\Delta\,@\,i_{1}\quad\Omega\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}A^{\prime}:\Delta^{\prime}\,@\,i_{2}}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\,;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}\left(A;A^{\prime}\right):\Delta\,;\Delta^{\prime}\,@\,i_{3}}\quad\textsc{T-Args-Semic-1}\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}A:\Delta^{\prime}\,@\,i\quad\Delta\;\texttt{null}}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\,;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}\left(\cdot;A\right):\Delta\,;\Delta^{\prime}\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Args-Semic-2}\par\frac{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}A:\Delta\,@\,i\quad\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}A^{\prime}:\Delta^{\prime}\,@\,i}{\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Sigma}\left(A,A^{\prime}\right):\Delta\,,\Delta^{\prime}\,@\,i}\quad\textsc{T-Args-Comma}\par

Buffering Rules

The left rules for star and sums, as well as Wait, include a
buffer
in the term: a prefix of the input context, where we store inputs until
we have received enough to run the term. For example, the
Wait
rule
has this buffer
\eta
, which we gather until it includes a maximal prefix of
x:s
.

\par\frac{\eta\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma(x:s)\right)\quad\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma(x:s)\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}\quad\Omega,x:\langle s\rangle\mid\Gamma(\cdot)\vdash_{\Sigma}e:s\,@\,ii^{\prime}=\texttt{I}\implies\neg\left(\eta(z)\;\texttt{maximal}\right)\wedge\neg\left(s\;\texttt{null}\right)}{\Omega\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\Sigma}\texttt{wait}_{\eta,t}(x)\left(e\right):t\,@\,i^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{T-Wait}

The buffer is included in the syntax of the term. Additionally, the context in the conclusion is
\delta_{p}\left(\Gamma(\Delta)\right)
.
If we’ve buffered
\eta
of the input, the term is expecting the rest of the context.
Users of the calculus need not worry about this detail: when writing programs and when the program starts running,
the buffer is empty:
\eta=\texttt{emp}_{\Gamma(\Delta)}
, and since

\delta_{\eta}\left(\Gamma(\Delta)\right)=\Gamma(\Delta)
, this returns
Wait
to the expected rule presented
in the body of the paper. The other rules that include buffers are
Plus-L
and
Star-L
.

C.8.
Sink Terms

Once we have produced an entire maximal prefix
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
, a program
e
of type
s

needs to transition to a program emitting nothing: we compute this term from
p
with
\texttt{sink}_{p}
.

Definition C.51 (Sink Terms).

We define a term
\texttt{sink}_{p}
by induction on
p
.

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{epsEmp}}=\texttt{sink}

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{oneEmp}}=\texttt{sink}

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{oneFull}}=\texttt{sink}

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{parPair}(p_{1},p_{2})}=\left(\texttt{sink}_{p_{1}},\texttt{sink}_{p_{2}}\right)

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{catFst}(p)}=\texttt{sink}_{p}

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{catBoth}(p_{1},p_{2})}=\texttt{sink}_{p_{2}}

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{sumEmp}}=\texttt{sink}

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{sumInl}(p)}=\texttt{sink}_{p}

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{sumInr}(p)}=\texttt{sink}_{p}

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{starEmp}}=\texttt{sink}

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{starDone}}=\texttt{sink}

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{starFirst}(p)}=\texttt{sink}_{p}

•

\texttt{sink}_{\texttt{starRest}(p,p^{\prime})}=\texttt{sink}_{p^{\prime}}

Note that (because it’s easier to have this be a function rather than a relation) sink terms are defined for
all
prefixes
rather than just the maximal ones.

Sink terms are closed, and have the type we expect for a stream transformer that has just emitted an maximal
p
of type
s
.

Theorem C.52 (Sink Terms Typing).

If
p\;\texttt{maximal}
and
p\,:\,\texttt{prefix}\left(s\right)
and
\delta_{p}\left(s\right)\sim s^{\prime}
, then

\cdot\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\emptyset}\texttt{sink}_{p}:s^{\prime}\,@\,\texttt{I}

The relevant concatenation property of sink terms is that they only depend on the the shape of
the type
s

after
the prefix has been emitted, so adding more to the beginning does not change anything.

Theorem C.53 (Sink Term Concatenation).

If
p\cdot p^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}
, then

\texttt{sink}_{p^{\prime}}=\texttt{sink}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
.

Proof.

By induction on
p\cdot p^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

Theorem C.54 (Fixpoint Substitution).

For
e,e^{\prime}
terms, we define
e\left[e^{\prime}/\texttt{rec}\right]
compositionally over the structure of
e
, with
the only two interesting cases being:

\left(\texttt{rec}\left\{\overline{M}\right\}\left(A\right)\right)\left[e^{\prime}/\texttt{rec}\right]=\texttt{fix}\left\{\overline{M}\right\}\left(A\left[e^{\prime}/\texttt{rec}\right]\right).\left(e^{\prime}\right)

and

\left(\texttt{fix}\left\{\overline{M}\right\}\left(A\right).\left(e\right)\right)\left[e^{\prime}/\texttt{rec}\right]=\texttt{fix}\left\{\overline{M}\right\}\left(A\left[e^{\prime}/\texttt{rec}\right]\right).\left(e\right)

We define this mutually with a substitution for arguments
A
, with
A\left[e^{\prime}/\texttt{rec}\right]
defined compositionally over the structure of
A
.

Then if
\Omega\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\Omega|\Gamma\to s\,@\,i^{\prime}}e^{\prime}:s\,@\,i^{\prime}
,
we have:

(1)

If
\Omega^{\prime}\mid\Delta\vdash_{\Omega|\Gamma\to s\,@\,i^{\prime}}e:t\,@\,i
then
\Omega^{\prime}\mid\Delta\vdash_{\cdot}e\left[e^{\prime}/\texttt{rec}\right]:t\,@\,i

(2)

If
\Omega^{\prime}\mid\Delta\vdash_{\Omega|\Gamma\to s\,@\,i^{\prime}}A:\Gamma^{\prime}\,@\,i
, then
\Omega^{\prime}\mid\Delta\vdash_{\cdot}A\left[e^{\prime}/\texttt{rec}\right]:\Gamma^{\prime}\,@\,i

Proof.

(1) and (2) are proved by a routine simultaneous induction on typing derivations.
∎

C.9.
Semantics

Definition C.55 (Semantics).

We define relations
p\Rightarrow e\downarrow^{n}e^{\prime}\Rightarrow p^{\prime}
(as shown in
Figures
9
and
10
), and

\eta\Rightarrow A@\Gamma\downarrow^{n}A^{\prime}\Rightarrow\eta^{\prime}
(as shown in Figure
11
).

\par\frac{}{\eta\Rightarrow\texttt{sink}\downarrow^{n}\texttt{sink}\Rightarrow\texttt{epsEmp}}\quad\textsc{S-Eps-R}\par\frac{}{\eta\Rightarrow\texttt{()}\downarrow^{n}\texttt{sink}\Rightarrow\texttt{oneFull}}\quad\textsc{S-One-R}\par\frac{\eta(x)\mapsto p}{\eta\Rightarrow x\downarrow^{n}x\Rightarrow p}\quad\textsc{S-Var}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\
\par\frac{\eta\Rightarrow e_{1}\downarrow^{n_{1}}e_{1}^{\prime}\Rightarrow p_{1}\quad\eta\Rightarrow e_{2}\downarrow^{n_{2}}e_{2}^{\prime}\Rightarrow p_{2}}{\eta\Rightarrow\left(e_{1},e_{2}\right)\downarrow^{n_{1}+n_{2}}\left(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime}\right)\Rightarrow\texttt{parPair}(p_{1},p_{2})}\quad\textsc{S-Par-R}\par\frac{\eta\Rightarrow e_{1}\downarrow^{n}e_{1}^{\prime}\Rightarrow p\quad\neg\left(p\;\texttt{maximal}\right)}{\eta\Rightarrow\left(e_{1};e_{2}\right)\downarrow^{n}\left(e_{1}^{\prime};e_{2}\right)\Rightarrow\texttt{catFst}(p)}\quad\textsc{S-Cat-R-1}\par\vskip 7.3194pt\\

\frac{\overline{M}\downarrow\theta\quad\eta\Rightarrow\texttt{let}\,\Gamma=A\,\texttt{in}\,e\left[e/\texttt{rec}\right][\theta]\downarrow^{n}e^{\prime}\Rightarrow p}{\eta\Rightarrow\texttt{fix}\left\{\overline{M}\right\}\left(A\right).\left(e\right)\downarrow^{n+1}e^{\prime}\Rightarrow p}\quad\textsc{S-Fix}\par\frac{\eta\Rightarrow A@\Gamma\downarrow^{n_{1}}A^{\prime}\Rightarrow\eta^{\prime}\quad\eta^{\prime}\Rightarrow e\downarrow^{n_{2}}e^{\prime}\Rightarrow p\quad\delta_{\eta^{\prime}}\left(\Gamma\right)\sim\Gamma^{\prime}}{\eta\Rightarrow\texttt{let}\,\Gamma=A\,\texttt{in}\,e\downarrow^{n_{1}+n_{2}}\texttt{let}\,\Gamma^{\prime}=A^{\prime}\,\texttt{in}\,e^{\prime}\Rightarrow p}\quad\textsc{S-ArgsLet}\par

Recursive Argument Semantics

The arguments semantics
\eta\Rightarrow A@\Gamma\downarrow^{n}A^{\prime}\Rightarrow\eta^{\prime}
accepts an
environment
\eta
and runs it through
A
to produce an environment

\eta^{\prime}\,:\,\texttt{env}\left(\Gamma\right)
. This relation is essentially the same as evaluating
a large nested tree
T-Cat-R

T-Par-R
terms, structured
like the context
\Gamma
. The only difference is that, because context
derivatives do not remove the left component of a semicolon context (the

\Gamma
in
\Gamma\,;\Delta
) after a maximal prefix has arrived, we have a special term former
\left(\cdot;A^{\prime}\right)

for cat-pair terms
\left(A;A^{\prime}\right)
that have crossed over.
The context is required in the semantics so we can compute the empty environment in
S-Args-Semic-1-1
and
S-Args-Semic-2
.

Semantics of Buffering

The semantics for
Plus-L
and
Star-L

and
Wait
buffer in their inputs until enough
of the input has arrived to run the term, where the particular value of “enough”
depends on the rule in question.

To illustrate, consider the rules for Wait (
S-Wait-1
and
S-Wait-2
in Figure
10
).
In both cases, we take the incoming environment
\eta
, and concatenate it onto
the buffer
\eta^{\prime}
, to get the combined
prefix
\eta^{\prime\prime}
. We then dispatch on whether
\eta^{\prime\prime}
is enough input to run the
continuation
e
. In this case, “enough” means that
\eta^{\prime\prime}
contains a
maximal prefix
p
of
x:s
. If it does (
P-Wait-2
), we run the continuation,
substituting the maximal prefix in for the (historical) occurrences of
x
. If it does not, we simply save
\eta^{\prime\prime}
as the
new buffer in the resulting
wait
term, and return the empty prefix in
P-Wait-1
.

The semantics for
Plus-L
and
Star-L
are similar:
in all cases, we add the incoming prefix to the buffer, and then project
from the buffer. If not enough data has arrived, we return the empty prefix
and step to the same term but with an updated buffer.

Maximal Semantics Theorem

If all input prefixes are maximal and the step terminates, then the output
prefixes are maximal. The contrapositive of this fact is crucial: if the output
of a step is not maximal, than some stream in the input must still be sending more
data.

Lemma C.56 (Maximal Semantics Auxiliary).

(1)

If
\eta\Rightarrow e\downarrow e^{\prime}\Rightarrow p
and
\eta\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,e

we have that
p\;\texttt{maximal}
.

(2)

If
\eta\Rightarrow A@\Gamma\downarrow e^{\prime}\Rightarrow\eta^{\prime}
and
\eta\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,A
then
\eta^{\prime}\,\texttt{maximalOn}\,\Gamma

By mutual induction on the derivation of
\eta\Rightarrow e\downarrow e^{\prime}\Rightarrow p

and
\eta\Rightarrow A@\Gamma\downarrow e^{\prime}\Rightarrow\eta^{\prime}
.
